Yeah, if I spot them—even just across the hallway—I just completely freeze up.\n\nDoctor: Do you freeze up or feel anxious when you see things other than balloons, like the glitter you mentioned?\n\nPatient: Oh, no, the glitter just makes me annoyed because of the mess. It's not scary at all. I don't freeze up about anything else, really, just the balloons. \n\nI mean, logically, I know a balloon in the classroom is probably just a decoration. But when I see one... especially when it's stretched really tight, or it's just bobbing around near the children, it feels like it could burst right in my face. I get scared in a way that just seems way, way bigger than the actual situation calls for. \n\nWhich is tough because kids are obsessed with them. We had this one little boy, Leo, last year, who brought in this giant foil one for his birthday. Actually, the foil ones aren't quite as bad as the rubbery latex ones. Latex is just the worst. Did you know there was a huge latex shortage a while back? I remember trying to buy disposable gloves for the classroom during the pandemic and we couldn't find them anywhere. We had to use those weird, loose plastic food-prep gloves that slide right off your hands. It made helping the kids with finger painting an absolute disaster. We got blue paint all over the reading rug, and the janitor, Mr. Henderson, was so upset with me. He's retiring next month, actually, and they're throwing him a party in the gym... \n\nWait, I'm sorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, the anxiety. But yeah, no, it's really just the balloons that make me feel that way, not the glitter or anything else.\n\nDoctor: Has anything scary or traumatic ever happened to you involving a balloon?\n\nPatient: Well, yeah, actually. It started about seven months ago. We were having this big children's art celebration at the school, and they had all these balloons tied to the chairs. One of them just popped right next to my face. It was so loud, I swear my ears were ringing for an hour. \n\nWhich was really bad timing because right after that, I had to meet with the parents of one of my students, Chloe. Chloe is such a sweet girl, but she has this habit of chewing on her hair, and her mom was really worried about it. Her mom is an interior designer, actually, and she helped us pick out the new colors for the staff lounge. We went with this really calming sage green, which is supposed to be good for reducing stress, you know? Not that it helps much when the coffee machine is broken. We've been putting in requests to get it fixed for three weeks, and the district just keeps saying they're looking into it. I've had to start bringing my own thermos from home, but it never stays hot past lunchtime. I got it at Target, one of those stainless steel ones... \n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, right. Yeah, so ever since that art celebration seven months ago when it popped in my face, that's when this whole thing started.\n\nDoctor: Has your fear of balloons affected your ability to teach or interact with your kindergarten class in any significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it really has started affecting my work. Like, for normal classroom celebrations, I just can't be in the room if parents bring them in. I have to hand all of that off to my aides. Thank goodness for my main aide, Sarah. She is an absolute lifesaver. She's actually studying for her master's in early childhood education right now, which is amazing because the coursework has changed so much since I got my bachelor's. I remember we had to make these massive, heavy binder portfolios for our final projects, but Sarah does everything digitally on her iPad. She has this adorable floral case for it that she found on Etsy. \n\nI love Etsy, but it's so dangerous for my wallet, you know? I bought these custom wooden name tags for the kids' cubbies on there a few weeks ago, and the shipping took absolutely forever because they were coming all the way from Canada. The tracking just stopped updating for like ten days, and I was so worried they got lost in the mail. I had to message the seller, and she was super sweet about it, but... \n\nWait, sorry, what was the question? Right, teaching. So, yeah, handing things off to Sarah. But it's getting more obvious because I actually missed helping with a festival art table that I had planned myself. I spent weeks organizing all the crafts for it, but I couldn't go near it because they set up a giant balloon arch right next to my station. My supervisor definitely noticed I wasn't there and asked me about it later. So, yeah, it's really getting in the way of my job.\n\nDoctor: Are you avoiding any social or family gatherings because of the possibility of encountering balloons?\n\nPatient: Yeah, outside of work, too. I've definitely been avoiding things. Like, I completely skipped my niece's birthday party last weekend because I knew there'd be balloons everywhere. My sister always goes all out for parties. She actually hired a petting zoo for it this year! Can you imagine? A petting zoo in a suburban backyard. They had these little pygmy goats that are just the cutest things. I've always wanted a goat, but my husband says our homeowner's association would absolutely throw a fit. They're so strict about everything. Last month, they sent us a warning letter because our trash cans were left out two hours past the deadline. Two hours! It's ridiculous. My neighbor, Mrs. Gable, she got fined fifty dollars because her mailbox was painted the wrong shade of white. I didn't even know there were different shades of white for mailboxes, but apparently there's \"Navajo White\" and \"Antique White\" and... \n\nOh, gosh, sorry, I lost track again. What did you ask? Right, avoiding things. So yeah, I skip parties when I can. But at work, like I was saying, lately I’ve been asking my aides to handle the birthday circles or I move away from the festival tables if there are balloons nearby. And if I absolutely have to stay in the room, like if we're short-staffed or something, I just keep my distance as much as possible and focus on getting through it without looking at them. It's exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you see balloons?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. What brings me in is that the second I spot balloons at school, even across the hallway, my chest tightens up and I just freeze for a moment before I can even think clearly about what I'm supposed to be doing. It's like my heart just starts pounding out of nowhere. \n\nIt actually reminds me of this time I had way too much caffeine before a parent-teacher conference. I had stopped at this new coffee shop that opened up near my house—The Daily Grind, I think it's called? They have these amazing blueberry scones, but their espresso is so strong. I ordered a double shot because I had stayed up late grading these little fine motor skill worksheets. We've been working on cutting along dotted lines, and let me tell you, five-year-olds and safety scissors are a chaotic combination. Half of them end up cutting the paper into tiny little confetti pieces instead of following the lines. And then cleanup takes forever because those tiny scraps get stuck to the bottom of everyone's shoes. We had to ask the janitor, Mr. Henderson, to bring in the big vacuum last week. He's such a nice man, he always gives the kids high-fives in the hallway...\n\nOh, wait. Symptoms, right? Sorry. But yeah, my chest gets super tight, I freeze up, and I just can't think straight until I get away from them.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by your fear of balloons most of the time, even when you're not directly encountering them?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I'm overwhelmed *most* of the time, no. Like, if I'm just sitting at home watching TV, I'm not really thinking about them. We've actually been binge-watching this baking show lately, the one where they have to make these hyper-realistic cakes that look like everyday objects? It's wild. Last night, this guy made a cake that looked exactly like a pair of muddy hiking boots. Even the shoelaces were made of fondant or something. I can't imagine having that kind of patience. I tried baking a birthday cake for my husband last year, just a simple chocolate one from a box mix, and I somehow managed to burn the edges while the middle was still completely raw. Our smoke detector went off, and our dog, Buster, was howling for a good twenty minutes. He's a beagle mix, so his howl is just piercing. We adopted him from a rescue that brings dogs up from the South, and he's just the sweetest boy, even if he does steal socks from the laundry basket...\n\nSorry, what was I saying? Oh, right, being distressed. No, I don't really worry about them when I'm not around them. It's really just when I see them or know I have to be near them that it gets bad.\n\nDoctor: How long does your fear response to balloons typically last after you've seen one?\n\nPatient: It usually goes away pretty quick once I get away from them, honestly. Like, if I can just step out of the room or turn down a different hallway, my heart stops pounding after a couple of minutes. I just need to catch my breath and kind of reset. It reminds me of when I used to run track in high school. We had this coach, Coach Miller, who would make us do these awful sprint intervals in the middle of summer. It would be ninety degrees out, and we'd be running up and down the bleachers. I was never the fastest runner, but I always tried my best. My best friend at the time, Sarah, she was a natural athlete. She went on to get a full scholarship for cross country at some college out of state. We lost touch after a while, which is a shame, but you know how it is. People get busy, they move away. She actually moved to Colorado, I think. I've always wanted to visit Colorado, the mountains look so beautiful in pictures. My husband and I were talking about planning a trip out there for our anniversary next year, maybe renting a cabin or something... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, how long it lasts. Yeah, it doesn't last long at all once I'm out of sight of the balloons. Just a few minutes to calm down, usually.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your fear of balloons is affecting your overall quality of life or relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Well, my friends and family know about it, and they're pretty understanding, so it hasn't really ruined any relationships or anything. But work... yeah, it's starting to become a real problem. I've had to start handing off normal classroom celebrations to the teacher's aides. Like, any time there's a birthday, I just have to find an excuse to leave the room. \n\nAnd it's not just birthdays. Last month, we had this big spring festival at the school. I had to miss the whole thing and have someone else run my table. And my supervisor definitely noticed. She pulled me aside later to ask why I wasn't at my station, and it was just... it was really embarrassing. So yeah, it's definitely affecting my work.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work, where you feel like your fear of balloons is causing problems or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Outside of work, I can usually just avoid them. Like, I don't go to party supply stores, and if I'm invited to a kid's birthday party, I just make sure to ask the parents beforehand if there will be balloons. Most of my friends don't even have kids yet anyway. Actually, my friend Jessica just had a baby last month, a little girl. They named her Eleanor, which I think is so classic and sweet. They call her Ellie for short. I went over to drop off a casserole—a chicken enchilada one, my grandmother's recipe. It's so good, you use sour cream and green chiles in the sauce instead of the red sauce. My grandmother used to make it every Sunday when we'd go over to her house after church. She had this huge dining room table that could seat like, twelve people, and she always had these beautiful lace tablecloths that she brought over from Ireland when she immigrated. I don't know how she kept them so white with all of us kids running around spilling things. She was always so calm about everything...\n\nI'm sorry, I forgot what we were talking about again. Oh, other areas of my life. No, it's pretty much just work where it's causing real issues right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any hobbies or personal activities you've had to give up or avoid because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I've been spending a lot of time out in the yard lately because the weather has been so nice. I finally got my tomatoes planted. It's really just when I'm at work that it's on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having unwanted thoughts or images about balloons when you're not expecting to see them or think about them?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I get random images popping into my head or anything like that. She's having a boy, which is exciting because her husband comes from a family of all girls. They're planning to name him Theodore, but they're going to call him Theo. I think that's super cute. I bought them this adorable little onesie with a bear on it because, you know, Teddy bear, Theodore. I found it at this little boutique downtown that sells organic baby clothes. The owner is this sweet older lady who actually knits some of the sweaters herself. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I do. I mean, I know it is. I know a balloon in the classroom is probably just a decoration, you know? But when I see one stretched tight or bobbing near the children, it just feels like it could burst right in my face, and I get scared in a way that just seems way bigger than the situation actually calls for. It's embarrassing, honestly. Like, I'm an adult. I shouldn't be terrified of a piece of rubber. 

Speaking of rubber, I was actually looking for some new rain boots the other day. It's been raining so much this spring, and my old ones have a crack right along the seam. Every time I step in a puddle, my left sock gets completely soaked. It's the worst feeling, having wet socks while you're trying to teach. I was browsing online and I found these really cute yellow ones, like classic Paddington Bear style, you know? But they were sold out in my size. It's always the way, isn't it? Whenever you find something you actually like, it's either sold out or it doesn't fit right. My sister has this amazing pair of floral rain boots she got at a gardening store, of all places. She has this huge garden in her backyard. She grows all her own tomatoes and cucumbers. Last summer, she had so many tomatoes she was practically begging people to take them. She tried making her own salsa, but she accidentally used way too many jalapeños, and it was so spicy my husband's face turned completely red when he tried it. He loves spicy food, but even he had to drink a whole glass of milk...

Oh, I'm doing it again. Sorry. But yes, to answer your question, I do know the fear is excessive. It just feels so real in the moment.

Just when they're actually there in front of me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that the foil balloons aren't as bad as the latex ones, but do you react with fear to all types of balloons, or is it mainly just the latex ones that trigger this response?\n\nPatient: Well, it's really just the latex ones. The foil ones, you know, the shiny Mylar ones people get for birthdays or get-well-soons, those don't bother me much at all. They don't have that awful squeaky sound, and they don't pop the same way. The latex ones are the real problem. It's like, they're so unpredictable. My cousin actually had a huge bouquet of those Mylar ones at her baby shower last month. She's having a boy, which is exciting because her husband comes from a family of all girls. They're planning to name him Theodore, but they're going to call him Theo. I think that's super cute. I bought them this adorable little onesie with a bear on it because, you know, Teddy bear, Theodore. I found it at this little boutique downtown that sells organic baby clothes. The owner is this sweet older lady who actually knits some of the sweaters herself. I almost bought one of the sweaters too, but they were like fifty dollars, which seemed a bit steep for something he's going to grow out of in two months. Babies grow so fast, don't they? I swear, by the time the parents figure out what size they're in, they've already moved up to the next one...\n\nOh, I'm rambling again, sorry. But yeah, to answer your question, it's just the latex balloons that really get to me. The foil ones are fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of balloons is something that you cannot control and that is interfering with your daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, it really is interfering, honestly. I feel like I can't control it at all when they're around, and it's definitely affecting my work. Like, I have to hand off all the normal classroom celebrations to the aides now because I just can't be in the room if parents bring latex balloons. And last month, I had to completely back out of helping with this art table I had planned for the school's spring festival because they decorated the whole tent with them. My supervisor definitely noticed, she even asked me if I was feeling okay because I was hiding out in the teacher's lounge. Which, by the way, the lounge has been freezing lately. They changed the thermostat or something, and everyone is complaining. Mrs. Higgins, the second-grade teacher, actually brought in a space heater, but then the custodian told her it was a fire hazard and made her take it home. So now we all just sit in there drinking coffee to stay warm. The coffee machine is terrible, though. It's one of those old drip ones that burns the bottom of the pot if you leave it on too long. I usually try to stop at Starbucks on my way in, but the line in the drive-thru has been insane since they released that new seasonal latte. I waited twenty minutes on Tuesday...\n\nWait, sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right, the fear. Yeah, it's definitely out of my control and it's making my job a lot harder than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your fear of balloons is unreasonable or excessive?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I know it is. I know a balloon in the classroom is probably just a decoration, you know? But when I see one stretched tight or bobbing near the children, it just feels like it could burst right in my face, and I get scared in a way that just seems way bigger than the situation actually calls for. It's embarrassing, honestly. Like, I'm an adult. I shouldn't be terrified of a piece of rubber. \n\nSpeaking of rubber, I was actually looking for some new rain boots the other day. But they were sold out in my size. It's always the way, isn't it? Whenever you find something you actually like, it's either sold out or it doesn't fit right. My sister has this amazing pair of floral rain boots she got at a gardening store, of all places. She has this huge garden in her backyard. She grows all her own tomatoes and cucumbers. Last summer, she had so many tomatoes she was practically begging people to take them. She tried making her own salsa, but she accidentally used way too many jalapeños, and it was so spicy my husband's face turned completely red when he tried it. He loves spicy food, but even he had to drink a whole glass of milk...\n\nOh, I'm doing it again. Sorry. But yes, to answer your question, I do know the fear is excessive. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I definitely try to avoid them whenever I can. Lately, I've been asking my aides to handle the birthday circles, or if there's a festival table with balloons, I just move away from it. If I absolutely have to stay in the room, I just keep my distance and try to focus on getting through it without looking at them. 

Which is hard, because they're so bright. You know, speaking of bright colors, my husband and I were looking at paint swatches for the guest room this weekend. We're trying to decide between this pale yellow and a really soft sage green. I kind of lean toward the green because it feels more calming, you know? But my mother-in-law—the one with the onion trick—she insists that yellow makes a room look bigger. She actually painted her entire living room this incredibly loud mustard color back in the 90s, and she never changed it. It's so intense, especially when the afternoon sun hits it. We were over there for Sunday dinner, and she made her famous pot roast. It's usually really good, but this time she tried a new recipe with balsamic vinegar, and it just tasted... off. My husband ate two helpings anyway just to be polite, but on the drive home he admitted he had a stomach ache. We ended up stopping at the pharmacy for antacids, and the line was out the door. Apparently, everyone in town needed something on a Sunday night. The pharmacist looked so exhausted. I always feel bad for people working retail on the weekends...

Oh, goodness, I'm rambling again. I'm sorry. But yes, I do avoid them. Or I just try to endure it from far away if I can't escape.

Like, for normal classroom celebrations, I just can't be in the room if parents bring them in. I have to hand all of that off to my aides. Thank goodness for my main aide, Sarah. She is an absolute lifesaver. She's actually studying for her master's in early childhood education right now, which is amazing because the coursework has changed so much since I got my bachelor's. But at work, like I was saying, lately I’ve been asking my aides to handle the birthday circles or I move away from the festival tables if there are balloons nearby. And if I absolutely have to stay in the room, like if we're short-staffed or something, I just keep my distance as much as possible and focus on getting through it without looking at them. It's exhausting, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience any physical symptoms like a racing heart, sweating, or trembling when you see balloons?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely do. What brings me in is that the second I spot balloons at school, even across the hallway, my chest tightens up and I just freeze for a moment before I can even think clearly about what I'm supposed to be doing. It's like my heart just starts pounding out of nowhere. \n\nIt actually reminds me of this time I had way too much caffeine before a parent-teacher conference. I had stopped at this new coffee shop that opened up near my house—The Daily Grind, I think it's called? They have these amazing blueberry scones, but their espresso is so strong. I ordered a double shot because I had stayed up late grading these little fine motor skill worksheets. We've been working on cutting along dotted lines, and let me tell you, five-year-olds and safety scissors are a chaotic combination. Half of them end up cutting the paper into tiny little confetti pieces instead of following the lines. And then cleanup takes forever because those tiny scraps get stuck to the bottom of everyone's shoes. We had to ask the janitor, Mr. Henderson, to bring in the big vacuum last week. He's such a nice man, he always gives the kids high-fives in the hallway...\n\nOh, wait. Symptoms, right? Sorry. But yeah, my chest gets super tight, I freeze up, and I just can't think straight until I get away from them.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling overwhelmed or distressed by your fear of balloons most of the time, even when you're not directly encountering them?\n\nPatient: I wouldn't say I'm overwhelmed *most* of the time, no. Like, if I'm just sitting at home watching TV, I'm not really thinking about them. We've actually been binge-watching this baking show lately, the one where they have to make these hyper-realistic cakes that look like everyday objects? It's wild. Last night, this guy made a cake that looked exactly like a pair of muddy hiking boots. Even the shoelaces were made of fondant or something. I can't imagine having that kind of patience. I tried baking a birthday cake for my husband last year, just a simple chocolate one from a box mix, and I somehow managed to burn the edges while the middle was still completely raw. Our smoke detector went off, and our dog, Buster, was howling for a good twenty minutes. He's a beagle mix, so his howl is just piercing. We adopted him from a rescue that brings dogs up from the South, and he's just the sweetest boy, even if he does steal socks from the laundry basket...\n\nSorry, what was I saying? Oh, right, being distressed. No, I don't really worry about them when I'm not around them. It's really just when I see them or know I have to be near them that it gets bad.\n\nDoctor: How long does your fear response to balloons typically last after you've seen one?\n\nPatient: It usually goes away pretty quick once I get away from them, honestly. Like, if I can just step out of the room or turn down a different hallway, my heart stops pounding after a couple of minutes. I just need to catch my breath and kind of reset. It reminds me of when I used to run track in high school. We had this coach, Coach Miller, who would make us do these awful sprint intervals in the middle of summer. It would be ninety degrees out, and we'd be running up and down the bleachers. I was never the fastest runner, but I always tried my best. My best friend at the time, Sarah, she was a natural athlete. She went on to get a full scholarship for cross country at some college out of state. We lost touch after a while, which is a shame, but you know how it is. People get busy, they move away. She actually moved to Colorado, I think. I've always wanted to visit Colorado, the mountains look so beautiful in pictures. My husband and I were talking about planning a trip out there for our anniversary next year, maybe renting a cabin or something... \n\nWait, what did you ask me? Oh, right, how long it lasts. Yeah, it doesn't last long at all once I'm out of sight of the balloons. Just a few minutes to calm down, usually.\n\nDoctor: Are you feeling like your fear of balloons is affecting your overall quality of life or relationships with family and friends?\n\nPatient: Well, my friends and family know about it, and they're pretty understanding, so it hasn't really ruined any relationships or anything. But work... yeah, it's starting to become a real problem. I've had to start handing off normal classroom celebrations to the teacher's aides. Like, any time there's a birthday, I just have to find an excuse to leave the room. \n\nAnd it's not just birthdays. Last month, we had this big spring festival at the school. I had spent weeks planning this really cute art table where the kids were going to make these little tissue paper butterflies. I had cut out all the pipe cleaners ahead of time, sorted them by color into these little plastic bins I found at the dollar store. I love that dollar store, by the way, the one over on Maple Street? The manager there, Debbie, she always saves the good craft supplies for me when they get a new shipment in. She has this huge orange tabby cat that just sleeps on the counter by the register all day. It's so funny, people just scan their items right over him and he doesn't even flinch. I tried to pet him once and he just gave me this look, like I was interrupting his very important schedule of napping. Cats are so funny like that, they just own whatever space they're in. My neighbor has a cat that keeps digging up my petunias, and I've tried putting down coffee grounds, but...\n\nOh, I'm sorry, I'm doing it again. The festival. Right. So, I had planned the whole art table, but when I got there to set up, the PTA had tied these massive bundles of balloons to the corners of my tent. I just couldn't do it. I had to miss the whole thing and have someone else run my table. And my supervisor definitely noticed. She pulled me aside later to ask why I wasn't at my station, and it was just... it was really embarrassing. So yeah, it's definitely affecting my work.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, besides work, where you feel like your fear of balloons is causing problems or making it hard for you to do things you want or need to do?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Outside of work, I can usually just avoid them. Like, I don't go to party supply stores, and if I'm invited to a kid's birthday party, I just make sure to ask the parents beforehand if there will be balloons. Most of my friends don't even have kids yet anyway. Actually, my friend Jessica just had a baby last month, a little girl. They named her Eleanor, which I think is so classic and sweet. They call her Ellie for short. I went over to drop off a casserole—a chicken enchilada one, my grandmother's recipe. It's so good, you use sour cream and green chiles in the sauce instead of the red sauce. My grandmother used to make it every Sunday when we'd go over to her house after church. She had this huge dining room table that could seat like, twelve people, and she always had these beautiful lace tablecloths that she brought over from Ireland when she immigrated. I don't know how she kept them so white with all of us kids running around spilling things. I spilled grape juice on one once when I was about seven, and I thought she was going to be so mad, but she just laughed and used some kind of club soda trick to get it out. She was always so calm about everything...\n\nI'm sorry, I forgot what we were talking about again. Oh, other areas of my life. No, it's pretty much just work where it's causing real issues right now.\n\nDoctor: Are there any hobbies or personal activities you've had to give up or avoid because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I mean, my hobbies are mostly things I do at home or outside where balloons aren't really a thing. I love gardening, for instance. I've been spending a lot of time out in the yard lately because the weather has been so nice. I finally got my tomatoes planted. I'm trying this new heirloom variety this year, they're supposed to be these really big, purple ones. I read about them in this gardening magazine I picked up at the dentist's office. My dentist, Dr. Evans, he has the best magazines in his waiting room. Not just the usual old gossip ones, but like, architectural digest and national geographic. Last time I was there, I read this fascinating article about these deep-sea vents and the weird creatures that live down there in the dark. It's crazy to think about what's at the bottom of the ocean, you know? It's like a whole alien world right here on Earth. I watched a documentary about it once with my husband, and he fell asleep halfway through, but I was just glued to the screen. He always falls asleep during movies, it's a running joke between us. We went to see that new superhero movie last weekend and he was snoring before the bad guy even showed up...\n\nWait, what was the question? Oh, hobbies. Yeah, no, I haven't had to give up any hobbies. Gardening, reading, cooking... none of that really involves balloons, thankfully.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself thinking about or worrying about balloons even on days when you don't see any?\n\nPatient: Um, no, not really. I don't just sit around worrying about them if they're not there. If I'm at home or just going about my day and there's no reason for a balloon to be around, it doesn't even cross my mind. I try to keep my mind busy anyway. Like yesterday, I spent the whole afternoon organizing my pantry. I bought these clear acrylic bins online—they were on sale, actually, which was great because usually they're so expensive. I got a set of eight for like, half price. Anyway, I took everything out, wiped down all the shelves with this lavender-scented cleaner I make myself. It's just water, a little bit of white vinegar, and a few drops of essential oil. It smells so much better than the harsh chemical stuff you buy at the store. My sister-in-law got me into making my own cleaners. She's super into all that natural living stuff, she even makes her own laundry detergent out of castile soap and washing soda. I tried it once, but I don't know, I just felt like my clothes didn't get quite as clean. Especially my husband's work shirts, he's a mechanic, so he gets this grease on them that is just impossible to get out. We have to use this heavy-duty degreaser on his laundry, otherwise...\n\nI'm doing it again, aren't I? Sorry. What did you ask? Oh, worrying about them. No, if I don't see them, I don't really think about them.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have days where you don't think about balloons at all?\n\nPatient: Yeah, oh definitely. On the weekends, especially, if we're just hanging around the house, I don't think about them at all. Weekends are usually pretty relaxed for us, unless my husband decides to start a project on one of his project cars. He's got this old '67 Mustang sitting in the garage that he's been \"restoring\" for about five years now. He says he's waiting on a specific part for the carburetor, but honestly, I think he just likes tinkering with it more than he actually wants to finish it. Which is fine, it keeps him out of trouble, I guess. Though last weekend he managed to track oil all over the kitchen floor. I had just mopped it, too, with that lavender cleaner I was telling you about. I had to scrub it for like twenty minutes to get the grease up. We ended up just ordering pizza for dinner because I was too tired to cook after all that cleaning. We usually get pizza from this little place down the street, Mario's. They make the best garlic knots. They're so buttery and covered in parmesan cheese. I could probably eat a whole box of them by myself...\n\nSorry, what were we talking about? Oh, right, days without thinking about balloons. Yeah, most weekends I don't even think about them. It's really just when I'm at work that it's on my mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself having unwanted thoughts or images about balloons when you're not expecting to see them or think about them?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn't say I get random images popping into my head or anything like that. Like I said, if they aren't around, they aren't really on my radar. My brain is usually too full of other stuff anyway. Between my husband's projects and my classroom, there's always something to plan or fix. I was actually just making a list this morning of things I need for the classroom next week. We're doing a whole unit on the life cycle of a butterfly, which the kids always love. I ordered these little mesh habitats online so they can watch the caterpillars spin their chrysalises. It's so magical for them. Though last year, one of the kids, little Tommy, he managed to unzip the habitat when I wasn't looking, and we had caterpillars crawling all over the reading rug. It took me an hour to find them all. Tommy's mom is one of the PTA moms, the really intense kind, you know? She always brings these elaborate, homemade, gluten-free, dairy-free snacks for the class parties that the kids won't touch. Last time it was these zucchini muffins that literally tasted like dirt. I ended up having to sneak the kids some graham crackers so they wouldn't go hungry... \n\nOh, gosh, sorry. Unwanted thoughts. No, I don't get those. Just when they're actually there in front of me.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned earlier that the foil balloons aren't as bad as the latex ones, but do you react with fear to all types of balloons, or is it mainly just the latex ones that trigger this response?\n\nPatient: Well, it's really just the latex ones. The foil ones, you know, the shiny Mylar ones people get for birthdays or get-well-soons, those don't bother me much at all. They don't have that awful squeaky sound, and they don't pop the same way. The latex ones are the real problem. It's like, they're so unpredictable. My cousin actually had a huge bouquet of those Mylar ones at her baby shower last month. She's having a boy, which is exciting because her husband comes from a family of all girls. They're planning to name him Theodore, but they're going to call him Theo. I think that's super cute. I bought them this adorable little onesie with a bear on it because, you know, Teddy bear, Theodore. I found it at this little boutique downtown that sells organic baby clothes. The owner is this sweet older lady who actually knits some of the sweaters herself. I almost bought one of the sweaters too, but they were like fifty dollars, which seemed a bit steep for something he's going to grow out of in two months. Babies grow so fast, don't they? I swear, by the time the parents figure out what size they're in, they've already moved up to the next one...\n\nOh, I'm rambling again, sorry. But yeah, to answer your question, it's just the latex balloons that really get to me. The foil ones are fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of balloons is something that you cannot control and that is interfering with your daily life in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean, it really is interfering, honestly. I feel like I can't control it at all when they're around, and it's definitely affecting my work. Like, I have to hand off all the normal classroom celebrations to the aides now because I just can't be in the room if parents bring latex balloons. And last month, I had to completely back out of helping with this art table I had planned for the school's spring festival because they decorated the whole tent with them. My supervisor definitely noticed, she even asked me if I was feeling okay because I was hiding out in the teacher's lounge. Which, by the way, the lounge has been freezing lately. They changed the thermostat or something, and everyone is complaining. Mrs. Higgins, the second-grade teacher, actually brought in a space heater, but then the custodian told her it was a fire hazard and made her take it home. So now we all just sit in there drinking coffee to stay warm. The coffee machine is terrible, though. It's one of those old drip ones that burns the bottom of the pot if you leave it on too long. I usually try to stop at Starbucks on my way in, but the line in the drive-thru has been insane since they released that new seasonal latte. I waited twenty minutes on Tuesday...\n\nWait, sorry. What was I saying? Oh, right, the fear. Yeah, it's definitely out of my control and it's making my job a lot harder than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe that your fear of balloons is unreasonable or excessive?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I mean, I know it is. I know a balloon in the classroom is probably just a decoration, you know? But when I see one stretched tight or bobbing near the children, it just feels like it could burst right in my face, and I get scared in a way that just seems way bigger than the situation actually calls for. It's embarrassing, honestly. Like, I'm an adult. I shouldn't be terrified of a piece of rubber. \n\nSpeaking of rubber, I was actually looking for some new rain boots the other day. It's been raining so much this spring, and my old ones have a crack right along the seam. Every time I step in a puddle, my left sock gets completely soaked. It's the worst feeling, having wet socks while you're trying to teach. I was browsing online and I found these really cute yellow ones, like classic Paddington Bear style, you know? But they were sold out in my size. It's always the way, isn't it? Whenever you find something you actually like, it's either sold out or it doesn't fit right. My sister has this amazing pair of floral rain boots she got at a gardening store, of all places. She has this huge garden in her backyard. She grows all her own tomatoes and cucumbers. Last summer, she had so many tomatoes she was practically begging people to take them. She tried making her own salsa, but she accidentally used way too many jalapeños, and it was so spicy my husband's face turned completely red when he tried it. He loves spicy food, but even he had to drink a whole glass of milk...\n\nOh, I'm doing it again. Sorry. But yes, to answer your question, I do know the fear is excessive. It just feels so real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever considered quitting your job as a kindergarten teacher because of your fear of balloons?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't really thought about quitting. I mean, I love teaching kindergarten way too much. The kids are just at such a fun age, you know? Like little Leo in my morning class, he made this incredible finger painting yesterday. It was supposed to be a butterfly, but it honestly looked more like a very colorful blob, which is totally fine, it's about the process, not the product. We were using this new washable paint that the district finally approved. Thank goodness, because last year we had this blue paint that stained absolutely everything. I actually ruined my favorite cardigan with it. It was this lovely cream color, cable-knit. I tried everything to get the stain out—baking soda, vinegar, even that expensive stain remover you see on TV. My mother-in-law swears by club soda for stains, but honestly, I think that's just an old wives' tale. She's always giving me these little domestic tips. Like last week, she told me to put half an onion in the fridge to absorb odors. I did it, and then my whole fridge just smelled like raw onion! Every time I opened the door to get the milk for my cereal, it was just... overwhelming. I usually eat Cheerios, but lately I've been on a granola kick. There's this honey almond one at the grocery store that has these huge clusters... \n\nWait, I'm so sorry, I completely lost track again. Quitting. Right. No, I wouldn't quit my job. I just really wish I didn't have to deal with this whole fear thing at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you consistently tried to avoid balloons or situations where you might encounter them over the past few months?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I definitely try to avoid them whenever I can. Lately, I've been asking my aides to handle the birthday circles, or if there's a festival table with balloons, I just move away from it. If I absolutely have to stay in the room, I just keep my distance and try to focus on getting through it without looking at them. \n\nWhich is hard, because they're so bright. You know, speaking of bright colors, my husband and I were looking at paint swatches for the guest room this weekend. We're trying to decide between this pale yellow and a really soft sage green. I kind of lean toward the green because it feels more calming, you know? But my mother-in-law—the one with the onion trick—she insists that yellow makes a room look bigger. She actually painted her entire living room this incredibly loud mustard color back in the 90s, and she never changed it. It's so intense, especially when the afternoon sun hits it. We were over there for Sunday dinner, and she made her famous pot roast. It's usually really good, but this time she tried a new recipe with balsamic vinegar, and it just tasted... off. My husband ate two helpings anyway just to be polite, but on the drive home he admitted he had a stomach ache. We ended up stopping at the pharmacy for antacids, and the line was out the door. Apparently, everyone in town needed something on a Sunday night. The pharmacist looked so exhausted. I always feel bad for people working retail on the weekends...\n\nOh, goodness, I'm rambling again. I'm sorry. But yes, I do avoid them. Or I just try to endure it from far away if I can't escape."
